{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{20.51889pt}{-30.43887pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{gathered}\quad.

(39)

In the following, we will show the linking between
W_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{\widetilde{L}}
with
W_{\beta}\in\mathcal{L}
is always trivial, namely

\frac{S^{*}_{\alpha\beta}S_{00}}{S_{0\alpha}S_{0\beta}}=1\,,\quad\forall\ W_{\alpha}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}\,,W_{\beta}\in\mathcal{L}\,.

(40)

It is a consequence of the fact that the line operators in the Lagrangian algebra are mutually local. If we further restrict
W_{\beta}\notin\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
and use
W_{\beta}
to construct a symmetric bi-twist operator, then it should commute with any local operator
\psi
constructed using
W_{\alpha}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
by lifting the bi-twist operator to the bulk and moving it past through
W_{\alpha}
.

To show
W_{\alpha}
braid trivially with
W_{\beta}
, we will make use of the following two properties of the Lagrangian algebra
Cong:2016ayp
. First, the quantum dimension of
\mathcal{L}
is defined as

\dim\mathcal{L}=\sum_{W_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{L}}n_{\alpha}d_{\alpha}\,,

(41)

where
d_{\alpha}
is the quantum dimension of
W_{\alpha}
. It turns out that
\dim\mathcal{L}
is related to the total quantum dimension
D_{\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{F})}
of the SymTFT
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{F})
as

\dim\mathcal{L}=D_{\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{F})}\,,

(42)

where the quantum dimension
D_{\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{F})}
is defined by

D_{\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{F})}=\sqrt{\sum_{W_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{F})}d_{\alpha}^{2}}\,.

(43)

Second, all the line operators
W_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{L}
are bosonic.

Recall that the modular
S
-matrix is given by

S_{\alpha\beta}=\frac{1}{D_{\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{F})}}\sum_{\gamma}\mathcal{N}_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}\frac{\theta_{\gamma}}{\theta_{\alpha}\theta_{\beta}}d_{\gamma}\,,

(44)

where
\theta
is the topological spin of
W
, which is the diagonal element of the modular
T
-matrix. If
W_{\alpha}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
, the fusion
W_{\alpha}\times\mathcal{L}
should only contains elements inside
\mathcal{L}
, otherwise there exists a
W_{\gamma}\notin\mathcal{L}
such that

\textrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{F}}(W_{\gamma},W_{\alpha})=\textrm{Hom}_{\textrm{SymTFT}}(W_{\gamma},W_{\alpha}\times\mathcal{L})\neq 0\,,

(45)

where we use (
21
). That means
t(W_{\alpha})
shares common elements with
t(W_{\gamma})
on
B_{\textrm{top}}
. Since
W_{\gamma}
must project to the non-trivial generator in
\mathcal{F}
on the
B_{\textrm{top}}
, we have a contradiction. Therefore
W_{\gamma}\in W_{\alpha}\times W_{\mu}\in\mathcal{L}
is also bosonic and we can set all topological spin
\theta_{\alpha},\theta_{\mu}
and
\theta_{\gamma}
equal to one and get

S_{\alpha\beta}=\frac{1}{D_{\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{F})}}\sum_{\gamma}\mathcal{N}_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}d_{\gamma}=\frac{d_{\alpha}d_{\beta}}{D_{\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{F})}}\,,

(46)

where we use the identity

d_{\alpha}d_{\mu}=\sum_{\gamma}\mathcal{N}_{\alpha\mu}^{\gamma}d_{\gamma}\,.

(47)

Therefore we have

\frac{S^{*}_{\alpha\beta}S_{00}}{S_{0\alpha}S_{0\beta}}=\frac{d_{\alpha}d_{\beta}}{D_{\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{F})}}\times\frac{S_{00}}{S_{0\alpha}}\times\frac{S_{00}}{S_{0\beta}}\times S_{00}^{-1}=1\,,

(48)

where we use
S_{0\alpha}/S_{00}=d_{\alpha}
and
S_{00}=D^{-1}_{\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{F})}
.

6
Examples

In this section, we will study three types of 2D examples and illustrate the analysis of the violation of additivity and Haag duality from the SymTFT perspective. We will begin with the simplest abelian group
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
and then move to general non-abelian groups and investigate
S_{3}
as a concrete example. Then, we will consider the diagonal RCFT whose symmetries are generated by Verlinde lines and recover the criteria in
Shao:2025mfj
.

6.1
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
symmetry

The first example is the
2D
anomalous free
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
theory
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{Z}_{N}}
. The SymTFT is the 3d BF-theory with level-
N

S=\frac{N}{2\pi}\int B\wedge dA\,,

(49)

where we have a pair of
U(1)
-valued gauge field
A
and
B
. The anyons are given by the gauge invariant Wilson line operators
W_{(\alpha,\beta)}[\Gamma]

W_{(\alpha,\beta)}[\Gamma]=\exp\left(i\oint_{\Gamma}\alpha A+\beta B\right)\,,\quad(\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{Z}_{N})

(50)

with
\alpha,\beta=0,1,\cdots,N-1
and
\Gamma
is a 1-cycle. The fusion rule is simply

W_{(\alpha,\beta)}\times W_{(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})}=W_{(\alpha+\alpha^{\prime},\beta+\beta^{\prime})}\,.

(51)

The Lagrangian algebras can be found by finding the maximal commuting set of operators. We have two basic Lagrangian algebras, the Dirichlet one and the Neumann one

\mathcal{L}_{\textrm{Dir}}=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}}W_{(\alpha,0)}\,,\quad\mathcal{L}_{\textrm{Neu}}=\bigoplus_{\beta\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}}W_{(0,\beta)}\,.

(52)

If
N
is not prime, for any positive integers
P,Q
satisfying
N=PQ
and
\gcd(P,Q)\neq 1
, one has others Lagrangian algebras

\mathcal{L}_{P,Q}=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}_{Q},\beta\in\mathbb{Z}_{P}}W_{(P\alpha,Q\beta)}\,,

(53)

and the Dirichlet (Neumann) algebra is the special case when
P=1
(
Q=1
).

Begin with the topological boundary
B_{\textrm{Dir}}
where the anyons
W_{(\alpha,0)}
can end on that, it supports a
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
symmetry generated by
W_{(0,1)}
. The Neumann boundary
B_{\textrm{Neu}}
is obtained by gauging the
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
symmetry generated by
W_{(0,1)}
and the corresponding Lagrangian algebra becomes
\mathcal{L}_{\textrm{Neu}}
. The role between
W_{(\alpha,0)}
and
W_{(0,\beta)}
are exchanged on
B_{\textrm{Neu}}
such that
W_{(0,\alpha)}
can end on the boundary while
W_{(1,0)}
serves as symmetry generator of the dual
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
symmetry. Moreover, if we only gauge a subgroup of
\mathbb{Z}_{P}\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}
we will obtain the topological boundary
B_{(P,Q)}
whose Lagrangian algebra is
\mathcal{L}_{(P,Q)}
. The symmetry supported on
B_{(P,Q)}
is
\mathbb{Z}_{P}\times\mathbb{Z}_{Q}
with a mixed anomaly between the two factors, generated separately by
W_{(1,0)}
and
W_{(0,1)}
.

For all of them, one finds

\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\textrm{Dir}}=\mathcal{L}_{\textrm{Dir}}\,,\quad\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\textrm{Neu}}=\mathcal{L}_{\textrm{Neu}}\,,\quad\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{P,Q}=\mathcal{L}_{P,Q}\,.

(54)

Therefore, the additivity is violated, and the Haag duality is preserved. It matches our result for invertible
\mathcal{F}
, i.e. when
\mathcal{F}
is a group.

6.2
S_{3}
symmetry

For a general discrete group
G
, the elements of its SymTFT
\mathcal{Z}(G)
are described by the pair
\alpha=(A,r)
where
A
labels the conjugacy class
Cl(G)
of
G
and
r
labels the irreducible representation under the centralizer group of
Cl(G)
.

Let us consider the non-anomalous order-3 cyclic group
S_{3}=\{e,a,a^{2},b,ab,a^{2}b\}
.
a
and
b
are the generators of
\mathbb{Z}_{3}
and
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
satisfying
a^{3}=b^{2}=1
and the constraint
ba=a^{2}b
. There are three conjugacy classes represented by
[e],[a],[b]

[e]=\{e\}\,,\quad[a]=\{a,a^{2}\}\,,\quad[b]=\{b,ab,a^{2}b\}\,,

(55)

and the centralizer group for
e,a,b
are separately

C(e)=S_{3}\,,\quad C(a)=\mathbb{Z}_{3}\,,\quad C(b)=\mathbb{Z}_{2}\,,

(56)

where
\mathbb{Z}_{3}
and
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
are generated by
a
and
b
separately. Recall that the irreducible representations for
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
are characterized by an
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
-integer
k
with

r_{k}(g)=e^{\frac{2\pi i}{N}k},\quad\textrm{($g$ is the generator of $\mathbb{Z}_{N}$)}

(57)

and the irreducible representations for
S_{3}
contain the trivial representation
r_{+}
, sign representation
r_{-}
and the 2-dimensional standard representation
r_{E}
. There are in total 8 line operators labeled by

([e],r_{+}),([e],r_{-}),([e],r_{E}),([b],r_{0}),([b],r_{1}),([a],r_{0}),([a],r_{1}),([a],r_{2})

(58)

and we shall label them as
W_{0},\cdots,W_{7}
in the following. The fusion rule is summarized in Table
.
